<meta>This page continues the analysis of the pictorial manuscript, focusing on the military campaigns and ritual investitures of the Mixtec hero Lord Eight-Deer "Jaguar-Claw" as depicted on pages 79 and 80 of the codex.</meta>

On page 79, in both cases, the scenes are accompanied by the calendar sign **Nine Grass** <note>original: "Nine Malinalli"; Malinalli is the Nahuatl word for a type of twisted grass used in the 260-day divinatory calendar</note>.

Above this sign, our hero stands in the posture of a conqueror, grasping the hair of a weeping warrior named **Two-Flower**. On his jacket, the captive displays a symbol that would be identical to the well-known sign for the town of **Chalco** <note>A powerful city-state in the Valley of Mexico</note> if not for an extra fifth circle. This same symbol appears on the thatched roofs of temples on pages 15, 18, and 19.

The poses of the victor and his prisoner are reminiscent of the carved stone groups found on the famous monument known as the **"Stone of Tizoc"** <note>A large, circular sacrificial stone carved during the reign of the Aztec Emperor Tizoc, depicting him and his warriors capturing various ethnic groups</note>.

Thirteen leaders follow them, carrying the <term>pantli</term> <note>A Nahuatl term for a banner or flag; it often functioned as a mathematical symbol for the number twenty or indicated the bearer was an official messenger or merchant</note>. These banners suggest the men were either merchants or ambassadors on a peaceful mission. Some of these figures wear animal masks. Most of them display a distinct horizontal nose-ornament pierced through the center of the nose, with some featuring a dangling turquoise stone on one side. It is noteworthy that during this campaign, **Eight-Deer** adopted and later wore this horizontal nose-piece, adding it to the turquoise nose-stud he received earlier in his life. <margin>On page 77, after listing four local place names, the artist shows the burning and destruction of a great temple, which signifies a total military victory.</margin>

The familiar figures of **Eight-Deer** and his loyal allies, **Twelve-Movement** <note>original: "Twelve-Ollin"; Ollin represents the concept of movement or earthquake</note> and **Four-Jaguar** <note>original: "Four-Ocelot"</note>, appear next in battle against two unusual masked warriors. Symbols of war—the shield and spears—appear twice on page 78. Two warriors are shown on one knee offering gifts, one of which looks like a gold bell of a common design. Finally, near a temple, we see **Eight-Deer** and a priest named **Seven-Dog** performing the ritual of "drilling the sacred fire." <note>The New Fire Ceremony was a vital ritual usually performed only once every 52 years to mark the beginning of a new calendar cycle and ensure the sun would rise again</note>.

On page 79, beneath a mountain marked with the sign **Nine Grass**, sits a leader accompanied by the signs **Eleven Grass** and **One Death**. Above them is a very significant group, nearly identical to a scene found in the **Becker Codex** <note>A related Mixtec pictorial manuscript now held in Vienna</note> on page IV. **Eight-Deer** and his ally **Four-Jaguar** kneel on top of a building. Both hold one hand up in a specific gesture while pointing with the other toward a basin at the top of the stairs. It appears as though blood is flowing from this basin down the steps. A blue band decorated with stars underneath the structure suggests it is connected to the "heavens" or the sky.

The following scene shows a seated leader presenting a blue ribbon with three gold bells to **Eight-Deer**, who points to the ornament and bows his head in respect. Above him is **Four-Jaguar**, who here and on the next page is shown with red stripes painted on his body. Similar examples of this body paint appear on pages 4 and 21.

The primary scene on page 80 shows three warriors in boats, all traveling in the same direction and holding up spear-throwers. The water they are navigating is drawn with large, curling waves and white foam; in the depths, the artist has included a large shell and a fish. The third leader is named **Nine-Movement**. His companions are identified as **Four-Jaguar** and **Eight-Deer**.

The group of the three chieftains in boats brings to one's mind a passage in the beautiful metrical poem in the <note>Nahuatl is the language spoken by the Aztecs and related peoples of Central Mexico.</note> Nahuatl language which the late Dr. Brinton translated as follows: "There were Rabbit <note>original: "Tochin"</note>, with many boats, the noble Puma-Shoulder <note>original: "Acolmiztlan"</note>, the noble Calocih, Night-Shiner <note>original: "Yohuallatonoc"</note>, and Little Lizard <note>original: "Cuetzpaltzin"</note> and White Coyote <note>original: "Iztac-coyotl"</note>, bold leaders from Tlaxcala <note>original: "Tlaxcalla," meaning "Place of Tortillas."</note>, and Serpent Lord <note>original: "Coatzintecuhtli"</note> and Huitlalotzin, famed as flowers on the field of battle." ^1

There seems to me to be no doubt that the Ancient Mexican reader of the present Codex <note>A "codex" is a hand-painted manuscript, usually folded like a screen.</note> recited in beautiful language, and metrical verse, composed by himself or acquired by oral transmission, the history of the native heroes whose deeds are pictured, accompanied by hieroglyphs recording the names of persons or of localities only. By this time those who have followed my outline sketch of the history of Eight-Deer <note>Eight-Deer "Jaguar Claw" (1063–1115 AD) was a powerful Mixtec ruler whose life is a central theme of this manuscript.</note> will have realized that the Codex does not contain what might be termed a consecutive, written text, but merely consists of pictorial representation of events, accompanied by such hieroglyphic names which were necessary in order to preserve them exactly and fix them in the memories of the native bards, who would constantly derive inspiration from the painted pages. Returning to our examination of page 80, we next recognize Eight-Deer seated in the conventionalized representation of the <term>ball court (original: *tlachtli*; a specialized I-shaped court used for both a ritual sport and ceremonial diplomacy)</term>, which was also employed for ceremonial purposes.

Although the shield and darts, the symbols of warfare, lie in front of Eight-Deer and his partner seated in the ball court, it is obvious that an amicable treaty and exchange of gifts is taking place. The two hieroglyphs which follow are particularly interesting because they occur elsewhere in relative proximity to each other. The lower one is but a larger form of that at the bottom of column 1, page 71. The counterpart of the second is in column 2, page 72. It represents a mountain from the summit of which smoke is rising, while steam issues from an opening lower down. The next page (81) records two deaths: that of Nine-Movement <note>original: "Nine-Ollin." Ollin represents movement or earthquake in the calendar.</note>, the occupant of the third boat on the preceding page, and of Eleven-Death, the occupant of the second boat on page 75, who was evidently sacrificed, as a prisoner of war, in a temple. Elaborate funeral rites were evidently held in honor of Nine-Movement, whose mummified form, in a decorated scaffolding, is represented at the top of the page. The familiar personage Twelve-Movement <note>original: "Twelve-Ollin."</note>, in priestly array, is setting fire to the funeral pyre with a lighted torch. Opposite to him a priest named One-Water presents the sacrificial bird. A group of funeral offerings follows, and five persons advance, respectively carrying a sacrificial bird and a bag or vessel in the form of a jaguar's foot; a vessel containing a fermented drink with flowers; a ceremonial, fringed garment; a blue bowl containing "the drink of life," or <term>pulque (original: *pulque*; a traditional alcoholic beverage made from the fermented sap of the agave plant)</term>; and a garland. The hieroglyph of a locality (*compare* page 74, column 2) follows at the top of page 82, above our hero Eight-Deer, who, like his companion Twelve-Movement, appears to have superintended the honors conferred upon Nine-Movement. The effigy of the latter exhibiting a blue mask, an elaborate head-dress, and a ceremonial garment, is depicted at the bottom of the page, and answers to descriptions given, by various writers, of the funeral effigies which were made of a log of wood, in the hollow of which the cremated remains were preserved.

In front of this effigy, which was erected on a structure made of <term>pine wood (original: *ocotl*; a highly resinous pine used for torches and sacred fires)</term>, memorial services were held at certain dates during four years, at the end of which time the effigy and cremated remains were again burnt and then

The so-called gladiatorial combat which took place at the annual festival <term>Tlacaxipehualiztli (The Flaying of Men)</term> <note>A major festival dedicated to the god Xipe Totec, involving ritual combat and sacrifice to ensure agricultural fertility.</note> is likewise represented by the weeping prisoner attached by a cord to the hollow centre of a circular stone, who is being attacked by two warriors wearing ocelot <note>Likely referring to jaguar skins and masks, as jaguars were the premier predators of the Mexican highlands.</note> masks. One of the latter is designated as **Eight-Deer**, who apparently officiated in this ceremony.

His heart is pierced by a spear, apparently thrown by the personage who wears a death’s mask, and is depicted at the bottom of the preceding page. The note in European script <note>Likely a later annotation by a Spanish friar or a colonial-era scribe.</note> also connects this sacrifice with the festival **Tlacaxipehualiztli**. It is interesting to note here that, according to the *Codex Telleriano-Remensis* <note>A 16th-century colonial manuscript that provides a calendar and history of the Aztecs.</note>, page 24, this mode of killing the victim was adopted for the first time by **Montezuma** <note>Also known as Moctezuma II, the Aztec emperor at the time of the Spanish arrival.</note>, in 1506. The present Codex apparently yields testimony that this rite was performed in the year **Twelve Tochtli** (1478), after a victory gained by **Eight-Deer**. The book closes with two groups, one representing the immolation of a bird and the burning of certain ceremonial offerings and of an arrow, possibly that which entered the victim’s heart, and the other showing the cremation of the skull and bones of the victim. Although these ceremonies form the natural conclusion to the episode depicted, the Codex is, as I have stated before, unfinished, its last page being only partly colored.

Let us now examine page 26, on which the first representations of **Eight-Deer** and of his comrade **Twelve-Ollin** <note>12 Movement.</note>, occur. The fact that one of his two pictures on this page represents him without and the other with a beard and nose-turquoise, reveals that references were made to two periods of his life in the text which was probably recited by the poet. This view is corroborated by the circumstance that in column 3 **Eight-Deer** figures for the only time in association with a woman named **Twelve-Serpent**, who wears the serpent insignia of a <term>Cihuacoatl (Snake Woman)</term> <note>A title for a high-ranking female deity or a powerful political office usually held by a man in Aztec society, but here referring to a noblewoman's status or name.</note>, is seated opposite to him in a <term>tecpan (palace)</term> <note>original: "tecpan"; a Nahuatl term for a lordly house or administrative center.</note>, or house of government, and offers him a drinking vessel. The date **Thirteen-Serpent** is placed between them. At the top of column 1, in another *tecpan*, a woman named **Nine-Eagle** is seated on the stool of chieftainship opposite to a chieftain named **Five-Cipactli** <note>5 Crocodile/Alligator.</note>. The *tecpan* depicted at the bottom of the page is occupied solely by a chieftainess named **Eleven-Water**.

It is a remarkable fact that, of the twelve individuals represented on page 26, nine are repeated together, in a group, on pages 42 and 43, as well as the year-dates **Six Tecpatl** = 1472 <note>6 Flint.</note>, **Seven Calli** = 1473 <note>7 House.</note>, and **Ten Calli** = 1489, in the same sequence.

<meta>This page continues the analysis of the Codex Nuttall's pictorial narrative, shifting from the general structure to the specific representation of women and the introduction of a primary figure, Lord Eight-Wind.</meta>

It is a remarkable feature of the present manuscript <note>original: "Codex"</note> that, whereas two women only are pictured on its reverse, its first forty-four pages contain no less than one hundred and seventy-six representations of women, nearly all of whom appear to pertain to the ruling class and to be filling responsible positions, on an equality with men. There is no native manuscript in existence which affords so much valuable material as the present one for the study of the position, customs, costume, and face-painting of the women of Ancient Mexico. What is more, it fully corroborates the documentary records concerning the existence of <term>gyneocracies: societies or government systems ruled by women</term> and actually pictures chieftainesses engaged in warfare, or parleying and entering into negotiations with war-chiefs, or else sharing with men, on terms of equality, such functions as those of high-priest or head-chief.<note>For documentary evidence concerning the existence of societies ruled by women in Ancient Mexico, see pages 194 and 60 of *The Fundamental Principles of Old and New World Civilizations*. In a specialized study <note>original: "monograph"</note>, currently in preparation, I shall present a study of Women in Ancient Mexico, with special reference to the present manuscript.</note>

Returning to page 1 of the manuscript <note>original: "Codex"</note> I shall now enumerate, as an aid in its examination, the persons of prominence on this and the following pages.

## THE LORD EIGHT-WIND <note>original: "Eight-Ehecatl"</note>

**T**HIS personage is invariably represented with a helmet which simulates an eagle’s head, and a face painted yellow, red, and black. He figures first on the bottom of page 1, in the act of issuing from a cosmical symbol. Behind him stands a priest named Twelve-Crocodile <note>original: "Twelve-Cipactli"</note>, who carries a staff in his hand, and a bundle of staffs on his back.

Eight-Wind’s <note>original: "Eight-Ehecatl"</note> next picture shows him on a symbol of water with one foot concealed in a central sign. Followed by Eleven-Flower <note>original: "Eleven-Xochitl"</note>, who carries a staff, he receives the homage of four individuals who advance towards him, respectively carrying a lighted torch, a sacrificial bird, and ceremonial garments. At the top of the page he figures again on the summit of a mountain, on which a monkey and other signs are painted. He is followed by Twelve-Crocodile <note>original: "Twelve-Cipactli"</note>, bearing a ceremonial staff, and is receiving the homage of four priests, the foremost of which, evidently a beginner <note>original: "neophyte"</note>, is designated as One-Reed <note>original: "One-Acatl"</note> and carries a bundle of wood. On page 2 Eight-Wind <note>original: "Eight-Ehecatl"</note>, with eagle insignia, figures on a mountain, with both of his followers. Six priests advance towards him. One of these (Two-Rain) wears the insignia of the rain god, and another (Five-Flower) blows a conch-shell. While the day-sign Eight-Wind <note>original: "Eight-Ehecatl"</note> figures in the middle of page 3 and in column 1, page 4, it is only in column 1, page 5, that it appears again in combination with the bird-man. The latter apparently figures in front of the temple at the bottom of the page, and in column 2, where the descending rain-god pours water over his seated figure. We next see him, in festive array, seated opposite to a woman of equal rank. A decorated bowl stands between them, and behind Eight-Wind <note>original: "Eight-Ehecatl"</note> is a temple on a mountain combined with a Monkey <note>original: "Ozomatli"</note>.

The next and final picture of <term>Eight-Wind<note>original: "Eight-Ehecatl". Ehecatl is the Nahuatl word for Wind, one of the twenty day-signs in the Aztec and Mixtec calendars.</note></term> represents him with a hollow sunken mouth, the conventional method employed to denote old age. He is seated in a temple on the "monkey-mountain" with which he has been associated throughout. The six elaborately costumed and painted women behind him are particularly noteworthy, and exhibit different seated postures, probably indicative of age, tribe, or rank.

<margin>1 The following extract from the *Annals of the Cakchiquels* <note>A 16th-century manuscript documenting the history and mythology of the Cakchiquel Maya people of Guatemala.</note>, describing the destruction of the Tukuches, is interesting in this connection: "There were four women who had armed themselves with lances and bows and taken part in the battle fully equal to four young men. The arrows launched by these heroines struck the very mat <note>The 'mat' signifies the throne or seat of authority.</note> of Chucuybatzin. Truly it was a terrible revolt which the chiefs made of old. The chiefs of the battle afterwards set up the images of these women before the buildings of the Zotzils and the Xahils, whence these women had gone forth" (p. 157).</margin>

The interest of this portion of the text centres on page 3, where an animated warfare takes place, in which women take a prominent part. In column 1, a woman standing on a mountain is armed with shield and darts and holds an <term>atlatl<note>A traditional Mesoamerican tool used to throw spears or darts with much greater force and distance than by hand.</note></term> or spear-thrower. Beneath her a female warrior, named Nine-Monkey <note>original: "Nine-Ozomatli". Ozomatli is the Nahuatl word for monkey.</note>, is being seized by her victor, whose body is painted with diagonal stripes of color—a tribal fashion which is frequently represented in this and the Vienna Codex <note>A screenfold manuscript from the Mixtec region, one of the few to survive the Spanish conquest.</note>. At the bottom of column 2 a woman leads a striped captive by a cord bound around his arm.¹

The seizure of two striped prisoners by warriors in Mexican costume is depicted above, and at the top of column 2 the sacrifice of a striped captive is represented, in combination with the hieroglyphic names of two localities.

The female captive, Nine-Monkey, apparently recurs on page 4, column 3, receiving the homage of a man holding a banner, and designated as Ten-Death. She next appears in elaborate garb, wearing the symbol of death, a skull, as a helmet, and accompanied by two composite glyphs <note>Symbolic figures used in writing.</note>, one partly formed by a skull, the other by a bird combined with a spindle. She figures again with the same costume and glyphs on page 10 at the top of column 2.

The limits of the present introduction forbid my entering into a discussion of other interesting portions of the text, which, beginning on page 1, appears to end with the continuous line on page 11.

The figures on the remainder of page 11 and on pages 12 and 13 differ in size from the preceding ones, and the text now introduces us to a different set of persons and names. The continuous line on page 33 appears to mark the end of the text, which begins on page 11.

A remarkable consecutive history, upon the representation of which the artist expended his utmost skill and labor, is comprised between pages 11 and 22.

A few bare indications will suffice, for the present, to guide and aid the reader.

<note>1 The triangular **<term>shoulder-cape</term>** <note>original Nahuatl: Quechquemitl</note> appears to have been an important and significant article of feminine dress. It may be seen worn by women of highest rank on pages 3, 5 (in this case it appears to be made of ocelot skin, cf. page 29, 31, and 32), 17, 19, 20, 23, 24, 27, 28, 29, 31, and 32. Triangular capes of identical design are represented as worn by a woman and also as figured next to her day-name, on pages 27, 28, and 29.</note>

...her kneeling, and the lord **Five-Flower** <note>Mixtec names often consist of a number and a day-sign from the sacred calendar</note> in rich attire seated behind her. They appear to have sacrificed a dog and a bird before the two old priests, who also figured in the preceding pages.

Under this scene the lady is again visible, in water, offering incense to a priest under whom a shell is painted and before whom is a jar with the emblem of the rain-god <note>likely the deity known as Tlaloc in Central Mexico or Dzahui in the Mixtec region</note>. These, as well as the priest behind her, are the same as those who attend the purification rite on the preceding page.

At the bottom of column 2, page 17, on a plain between two hills, we see officiating priests and a flat pyramid temple, on the summit of which is a lighted pyre surmounted by a vessel holding the sacred young maize shoot. Above this group is a diminutive representation of the serpent temple depicted on page 15, and two ceremonial staffs are planted in front of it. A priest blowing a conch-shell trumpet and two others with offerings are also there. The priests and priestess in column 1, page 18, appear to belong to the preceding page.

A fresh episode in which a priest and a lady, both designated as **Three-Flint**, figure in connection with the lord **Twelve-Wind**, begins in the second section of page 18. A priest designated as **Three-Flint** and another named **Twelve-Wind**, who carries a miniature house on his back, and is characterized by conventionalized puffs of smoke under his eye, descend on a cord which hangs from the supposed centre of the sky. In the open space above this is the familiar cosmical symbol and a man's and a woman's head, with the day-signs **Four House** and **Five Serpent**, which recur in a similar position on page 21.

Below these, in water, is a man wearing the serpent insignia, in the act of performing the same rite observed by the lady **Three-Flint** on page 15, in the presence of the priestess **One-Eagle**. Three priests carry offerings, and **Twelve-Wind** presents a smoking incense burner to a curious composite symbol, which figures also on pages 15, 17, and 19, and apparently in connection with the serpent temples. A serpent temple with a flat roof and the Chalco glyph <note>Compare with the *Vienna Codex*, page 48</note> and ceremonial staffs complete page 18.

Page 19 is double, and constitutes the most elaborate portion of the Codex. The first section contains a curious representation of the blue sky, studded with stars, from three divisions of which three figures descend. In the open space above, pointing at a miniature house and the fire-making instruments, we see the lord **Twelve-Wind**, recognizable by the smoke symbol under his eye. He is seated between two serpent men and the signs **Four** and **Eleven** <term>Cipactli</term> <note>original: "Cipactli"; the day-sign of the Crocodile or Earth Monster</note>, which recur in an analogous open space above three descending figures on page 21.<insert>1</insert>

As on page 18, a cord hangs from the imaginary centre of heaven, and on it the lord **Twelve-Wind** descends and appears for a second time, walking on its lower end. In both cases his eye is painted with puffs of smoke, and he carries the same miniature house on his back.

A priest designated as **Three-Flint** also descends from heaven under a symbolical receptacle containing water and a shell.

The larger section of page 19 is occupied by a great green mountain, which forms the background for a number of pictures of persons engaged in the performance of an extremely elaborate ceremonial.

The flat-roofed temple depicted on page 18 is repeated here, and crowns a pyramidal structure situated on the mountain. A woman stands in front of it under the sign **One Death**. A small symbolical vessel, containing water and a flint knife, is on the steps of the temple. The jar, decorated with the emblem of the rain-god <note>likely Tlaloc or a Mixtec equivalent like Dzahui</note>, which figures in the water ritual on page 17, rests at the base of the temple stairs. A path, with painted footsteps upon it, issues from the base of the temple, and on it advances a procession headed by a priest bearing a lighted torch, followed by another blowing a conch-shell trumpet. The principal figure in the procession is, however, a priest, who carries a woman on his back by means of a red and white burden-strap <note>original: mecapal</note> which passes around his forehead. It was thus that, in Ancient Mexico, a bride was conveyed to the house of the bridegroom, usually by a stalwart matron. A priest carrying a ceremonial garment advances towards this singular group, which is also faced by two seated priests holding offerings.

The whole procession wends towards an arch, painted with variegated diagonal bands of color. Under this kneel the lord **Twelve-Wind** and the lady **Three-Flint**, while streams of water are poured upon them from above by two priestesses holding painted bowls. The high priestess **One-Eagle** again presides over this rite, holding between her hands a curious curved symbol, resembling the hieroglyph of the town of <term>Colhuacan</term> <note>original: Colhuacan; a legendary ancestral place meaning "Place of those who have Grandfathers" or "Curved Hill"</note>.

Directly above the two priestesses is a **palace** <term>tecpan</term> <note>original: tecpan; a lordly house or administrative palace</note>, in the doorway of which, partly concealed by a colored drapery, the lord and lady are visible.¹ A solemn procession consisting of seven priests and a priestess proceeds along the flat summit of the mountain. Next to the **palace** <term>tecpan</term> stand two staff-bearers, facing each other.

The scene thus depicted recalls the following passage from the *Annals of the Cakchiquels*:

> “Immediately they gave him, as their chief, the signs of royalty. They seated him on the seat and royal throne. They washed him in the bath, the painted vessel. They clothed him with the robe, the girdle, and green ornaments. He received the colors, the yellow stone, the paint, the red earth, and thus he obtained the signs of royalty from the other families and tribes, as said our ancestors, oh, my children” (page 111).

Page 20 commences with the picture of the lord **Twelve-Wind** and the lady **Three-Flint**, both occupying seats of honor, their equality of rank being thus indicated.

The lady wears the serpent head-dress denoting her rank of **Woman-Serpent** <term>Cihuacoatl</term> <note>original: Cihuacoatl; a high-ranking title in Central Mexican politics and religion</note>. The greater part of the page is taken up with representations of tribal plants and of a combat in which the strange people painted with diagonal stripes appear to be the aggressive party. One of them, designated as **Eight-Ollin**, lies prostrate, and is taken prisoner by **Nine-Wind**, who wears a **Flint Knife** <term>Tecpatl</term> <note>original: Tecpatl</note> helmet. A striped warrior also makes a prisoner designated **Four-House**, who was evidently put to death, as his mummy appears immediately beneath him. The sign of the first prisoner, **Eight-Ollin**, is painted under the second mummy. The old priests **Ten-Malinalli** and **Ten-Rain**, whom we have seen together on previous occasions (pages 14, 15, and 17, etc.), appear close to the mummied dead. A composite sign of speech, issuing from **Ten-Rain’s** mouth, indicates that he sang, on this occasion, the song of **Seven-Flower**. On page 21 he

### Page 32

...performs a ritual dance, carrying a prisoner's head by its hair. On this page the sign **Four Alligator** <note>original: "Cipactli," the first day-sign of the ritual calendar, representing a mythical water monster or crocodile</note> is prominent (*cf.* page 19, section 1), and the lord **Twelve-Wind** appears again in priestly garb, bearing a miniature house on his back. He is escorted by **Three-Flint**, who, as on pages 18 and 19, section 1, is under the guise of a priest. These two personages recur on page 22 for the last time, in which case it is **Three-Flint** who carries the miniature house. Here ends all record of the sign **Three-Flint**, which repeatedly occurs between pages 14 and 18, in association with a woman whose history is linked to that of the lord **Five-Flower**. On pages 18 and 19, section 1, it is a priest who is designated as **Three-Flint**, and is associated with the lord **Twelve-Wind**. On pages 19 and 20, **Three-Flint** becomes a woman again, and undergoes a ceremonial rite with **Twelve-Wind**. On pages 21 and 22, there are two instances where the sign **Three-Flint** designates the priest again.

Whether, in the case of the male and female individuals named **Three-Flint**, we have to deal with distinct personalities or the story of a disguise or metamorphosis, such as the native sorcerers were supposed to be able to accomplish, it is impossible to surmise. At the same time it is evident that the text presents a history which is full of dramatic and romantic possibilities. It reveals that the lady **Three-Flint** attained the rank of <term>Woman-Serpent</term> <note>original: "Cihuacoatl," a high-ranking title often held by a supreme advisor or high priestess</note>, and became the consort of two lords, and that her marriage, lying-in, and installation as **Woman-Serpent** were attended by the most solemn and elaborate ceremonials, in which priests and priestesses officiated.

It may safely be assumed that this inspiring history, the outlines of which were painted in picture and picture-writing with so much skill and care, was also clothed in beautiful language and flowery verse, and was recited from memory by the native bards on festive occasions.

A fresh theme, also dated **One Reed** <note>original: "Acatl"</note>, **One Alligator** <note>original: "Cipactli"</note>, the native New Year's Day, seems to begin on page 22, and to recount the story of another **Woman-Serpent** named **One-Serpent**. She is represented as kneeling by flowing water, and as seated on a flat pyramid temple on the summit of a mountain opposite to a similar structure on which a chieftain rests. Deep water containing shells, in what appears to be a tank or reservoir, lies between the two pyramids, and the pictures may possibly record the building of some ancient water-works.

Page 22 and those which follow, contain numerous representations of male and female chiefs, standing or seated opposite to each other, sometimes installed in <term>palaces</term> <note>original: "tecpans," the administrative and residential buildings of the nobility</note> and engaged in amicable negotiations. Terms of perfect equality appear to exist between the sexes. An exceptional instance is that of a woman who, on page 24, is represented as kneeling in the <term>palace</term>, whereas the man to whom she is speaking is seated. The arrow sticking in the corner of the building above her indicates conquest, and her attitude may show that she was a supplicant and belonged to a conquered tribe. Two other exceptional representations of kneeling women and seated men occur on page 41.

Throughout the Codex the attitudes of the hands and fingers appear to be extremely expressive, and it is regrettable that our ignorance of Ancient Mexican sign language debars us from interpreting the gestures which, in many cases, appear to express numerals and forms of agreement.

Page 25 contains particularly interesting pictures of a man named **Two-Water** and a woman **Ten-Alligator**, who...

Page 42 introduces us again to the set of characters depicted on page 26, amongst whom is the lord <term>Eight-Deer</term> <note>the famous Mixtec conqueror Eight Deer 'Tiger Claw'</note>, whose history fills the reverse of the <term>Codex</term>.

## REVIEW OF THE CONTENTS OF THE CODEX

**T**HE foregoing indications and description of the main features of the text will have shown that the latter is mainly pictorial, and that <term>ikonomatic</term> <note>a writing system where images represent phonetic sounds, much like a rebus</note> writing is employed only for the registration of personal and local names. It is also evident that the text consists of several parts of unequal lengths, which deal with different people and episodes. The greater part of the reverse of the <term>Codex</term> consists of the simple enumeration of chieftains and conquered localities. Other parts of the text appear to furnish material for the chants or songs, such as were improvised or committed to memory by the native bards who were famed for their choice and beautiful language and flowing verse. <gloss>1</gloss>

---

<gloss>1</gloss> Although beings of celestial descent are sometimes figured, it is obvious that the text deals with real persons and is mainly historical. Priests are represented wearing, as was customary, the insignia of some of the chief divinities, such as the rain-god, <term>Tlaloc</term> (pages 21, 22, 25, 26, 30, 34, 36-39), or of <term>Quetzalcoatl</term> <note>the "Feathered Serpent," a god of wind and knowledge</note> (pages 38, 47, 66), yet the text is certainly not of a religious nature.

<summary>This page concludes one narrative section of the manuscript and begins another, tracking the movements of characters like Seven-Rain and Eight-Deer while establishing that the codex is a historical and genealogical record rather than a purely religious one.</summary>
<keywords>Nuttall Codex, Mixtec history, Eight-Deer, Tlaloc, Xipe Totec, Nahuatl names, ikonomatic writing, pre-Columbian manuscripts</keywords>

--- Page 44 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s introduction to the manuscript, moving from the methodology of identifying names to the broader historical context of the Aztec and Mixtec regions between 1468 and 1519.</meta>

34

It is, of course, impossible to present, within the limit of an introduction, the result of my attempts to identify the local and personal names contained in the **Codex** <term>A "Codex" is a hand-painted manuscript, usually made of bark paper or deerskin, used by Mesoamerican cultures to record history, genealogies, and rituals.</term> by carefully comparing them with all of those printed in other native manuscripts or mentioned in historical documents.

Postponing the publication of my investigations in these lines, which are as yet far from being finished, I shall merely state that I shall publish at intervals, and in the same form as the present introduction, short papers embodying the results of my observations and analysis of the contents of this and the **Vienna** and other **Codices**. In conclusion, I will now refer to certain passages of Friar Diego Duran's *History* <note>original: "Historia"</note>, because they convey valuable information concerning the conditions existing in Mexico about the period dealt with in the **Codex**, i. e. 1468–1519. The friar relates that, just as medieval Spanish literature abounds with descriptions of the military exploits of its national heroes, such as the **Cid** and **Count Fernan Gonzales** <note>These are legendary figures from Spanish history; the Cid is the hero of the Reconquista, and Fernán González was the first independent Count of Castile.</note>, so Mexican history, of the time of **Montezuma I**, rehearsed and described the great, valiant warriors who, with excessive exertion and difficulties, had conquered, subjugated, and exacted tribute from a number of towns, cities, and provinces, thereby increasing the wealth and power of Mexico and causing its name to be feared and reverenced. (Vol. II, p. 208).

The dominion of Mexico was, indeed, considerably extended during the reign of **Montezuma I**, which, according to Duran, began in 1440. The **Chalcos** and **Cuetlaxtecas** were conquered, and just before his death, which, according to various writers, occurred in 1469 or 1471, he was leading a campaign against the province of **Tlatlauhquitepec**.

Under his successor, **Axayacatzin**, who died in 1481, rebellion and warfare were general. After a hard struggle **Tlatelolco** was subdued and destroyed by the Mexicans who also conquered the **Matlaltzincos**.

The aggressive policy of **Tizoc** and **Ahuizotl** is well known — the latter extended his conquests as far south as **Tehuantepec**, and imposed heavy tributes upon the coast tribes. The titles of some of the leaders of this memorable campaign are recorded, but not their names. Friar Duran tells us that the Mexican historical records are so incomplete that it was even a matter of doubt whether the Tetzcocan ruler **Nezahualpilli** <note>The son of Nezahualcoyotl and the "Tlatoani" (king) of the city-state of Texcoco.</note> had ever led his forces in person, notwithstanding the fact that a picture existed which represented him fully equipped and holding a prisoner by the forelock. A scroll beneath displayed this inscription: “Here, in the war of **Huexotzinco**, **Nezahualpilli** took this captain prisoner” (Vol. II, p. 398). The native historian **Ixtlilxochitl** <note>Fernando de Alva Cortés Ixtlilxochitl was a descendant of the ruling family of Texcoco who wrote detailed histories of his people after the Spanish conquest.</note> also relates that at this period (A. D. 1500), “the Mexican warriors were so eager to subjugate lands and people that they considered themselves as idle and cowardly if they were not engaged in some campaign or conquest” (Chap. LXV.).

Speaking of the conquest of the **Huaxteca**, in which the warriors **Xochiquetzalli** and **Acapioltzin** distinguished themselves, the native historian states, that besides relating in their songs the conquests and adventures of the leader, the poets of that time praised not only his heroic deeds but also those of his brothers.

Under the reign of **Montezuma II**, various wars of conquest were also waged, but the names and titles of their successful leaders have not been handed down to us. It may yet be possible to positively identify **Eight-Deer** <term>Lord Eight Deer "Jaguar Claw" was a powerful Mixtec ruler (1063–1115); his life is a central theme in several surviving manuscripts.</term> and other heroes whose exploits are commemorated in the present **Codex**, and to precisely localize the events which

<summary>The author contextualizes the Codex by comparing Mexican military history and its great rulers—such as Montezuma I and Ahuizotl—to European epic traditions. He highlights the difficulty of identifying specific historical figures in the records but expresses hope that "Eight-Deer" and others can be verified.</summary>
<keywords>Montezuma I, Axayacatzin, Tizoc, Ahuizotl, Nezahualpilli, Eight-Deer, Tlatelolco, Matlaltzincos, Mexican Codices, Friar Diego Duran, Ixtlilxochitl, Aztec military history, Texcoco</keywords>

--- Page 45 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's analysis of the historical significance and linguistic structure of the Mexican Codex, moving from general history to the specific mechanics of indigenous writing systems.</meta>

...are pictured therein. Even if attempts to do this should not prove to be entirely successful, we know, at the present time, quite enough to realize that, during the period dealt with in the *Codex*, Mexican history could well have furnished inspiration for songs of conquest and adventure.

While the *Codex* thus causes us to keenly realize how little we know concerning the history and literature of Ancient Mexico and the names of its national heroes, it supplies us with a wealth of fresh knowledge, especially concerning the dress, ceremonial observances, and position of women. It also furnishes testimony of incalculable importance to all interested in the history of the development of the art of writing, for it definitely proves that, even in such a high order of historical document as this, the native scribes did not attempt to write out sentences by means of the <term>ikonomatic<note>A method of writing where a picture represents a specific phonetic sound rather than the object itself, essentially acting as a "phonetic picture."</note></term> method, but employed this merely for the recording of personal and local names.

The *Codex* constitutes, therefore, a most interesting and instructive demonstration of a transition stage in the evolution of native writing. In this the simple pictorial presentation of events is made to convey a wealth of information to the initiated, by means of such accessories as details of costume, symbolical signs and colors, and individual positions, while the names of persons and localities were definitely and permanently recorded by <term>rebus signs<note>A system using existing pictures to represent new words or syllables through the sound of their names, much like a puzzle where a picture of a "bee" and a "leaf" might mean "belief."</note></term>.

It is obvious that a full and complete interpretation of this composite text and its rehearsal in flowing sentences could only have been made by a skilled scribe or <term>improvisatore<note>original: "improvisatore" (Italian). A performer who composes and recites poetry or stories spontaneously; in this context, someone trained to "read" the images and turn them into an oral epic.</note></term>, or one who had memorized the verses pertaining to the pictorial text.

A fresh poignancy is added to a regret, universally felt, when it is realized that amongst the countless native books which served as fuel for the bonfire which was lighted, by order of <note>Juan de Zumárraga (1468–1548) was the first Bishop of Mexico. While significant in church history, he is often remembered by historians for the systematic destruction of indigenous Aztec manuscripts.</note>Bishop Zumarraga, in the square of <note>Also known as Texcoco, this was one of the most powerful and cultured city-states in the Valley of Mexico, famous for its grand library of codices before their destruction.</note>Tetzcoco, there were doubtlessly many as beautiful as this, the last addition to the brief list of *Codices* known to be in existence at the present time.

<summary>This page evaluates the Codex as a vital record of Ancient Mexican social life and a transitional artifact in the history of writing, using a mix of pictorial scenes and phonetic rebus signs for names. It concludes with a lament for the many similar manuscripts destroyed by colonial church authorities.</summary>
<keywords>Ancient Mexico, Codex, ikonomatic writing, rebus signs, Bishop Zumarraga, Tetzcoco, indigenous literacy, oral tradition, manuscript destruction</keywords>

# Page 84.

1 Reed <term>Acatl: One of the four "year-bearer" signs in the Aztec calendar, associated with the East and the color red.</term>
2 Rabbit <term>Tochtli: A year-sign associated with the South and the color yellow; it often represented fertility and abundance.</term>
3 Flint <term>Tecpatl: A year-sign represented by a sacrificial knife, associated with the North and the color white.</term>
4 House <term>Calli: A year-sign associated with the West and the color blue-green; it symbolized the domestic sphere and the setting sun.</term>

<gloss>The Flaying of Men</gloss> <note>original: "Tlacaxipeoli," a phonetic spelling of Tlacaxipehualiztli. This was a major twenty-day festival dedicated to the god Xipe Totec ("Our Lord the Flayed One"). The ritual involved the symbolic shedding of skin to represent the renewal of the earth and the sprouting of new vegetation, much like a seed loses its outer husk.</note>

<note>The accompanying illustration shows a figure tied to a wooden scaffold, a specific form of sacrifice known as "gladiatorial" or "arrow" sacrifice associated with Xipe Totec. The red skin of the figure and the presença of the 'xicalcoliuhqui' (stepped-fret) border denote the sacred and transformative nature of this ceremony.</note>

->**The Voyage Across the Sea**<-

On this page, the narrative shifts from the rugged mountains of the Mixteca to the open water. This sequence is one of the most famous in the <term>Codex Zouche-Nuttall</term><note>A pre-Columbian screenfold manuscript from the 14th century, documenting the history and genealogy of Mixtec rulers.</note>, showing a rare indigenous depiction of naval travel.

### The Great Crossing
In the upper portion of the page, three noble **warriors** steer their canoes across a sea rendered with curling turquoise waves and a variety of aquatic life, including fish and shells. Each warrior is dressed in elaborate ceremonial regalia, indicating that this is not merely a fishing trip but a formal expedition of conquest or high diplomacy.

### Sacred Dates and Places
The journey is framed by specific chronological markers:
*   The year **7 Rabbit** <note>In the Mixtec 52-year cycle, "7 Rabbit" serves as a precise temporal anchor for this event.</note>
*   The day **4 Jaguar**

The destination is marked by several <term>toponyms</term><note>Place-signs used in pictographic writing to name specific locations through icons like hills, rivers, or buildings.</note>. We see a prominent **Hill of the Altar**, characterized by a mountain glyph topped with a stepped ceremonial structure. This suggests the warriors are traveling to a coastal shrine or an island kingdom that held significant religious weight.

### Navigation and Power
The canoes are long and slender, decorated with patterns that may represent the specific lineages or "House" of the travelers. The warriors carry shields and <term>atlatls</term><note>Spear-throwers used to launch projectiles with great force.</note>, signaling that they are prepared to defend their claim to these distant lands. This maritime scene emphasizes the far-reaching influence of the Mixtec lords, extending their power beyond the valleys and into the coastal regions.

<summary>This page illustrates a significant naval expedition by Mixtec warriors across a sea filled with life, likely representing a journey to a coastal or island sanctuary in the year 7 Rabbit.</summary>
<keywords>maritime travel, canoes, 7 Rabbit, 4 Jaguar, Lord 8 Deer, toponyms, Mixtec navigation, warriors</keywords>

### The Assembly of the Twelve Ritual Lords

<insert>In the year 26</insert> <note>original: "anno 26." This is a later European addition, likely made by a Spanish collector or scholar to catalog the manuscript.</note>

This page presents a vibrant ceremonial procession, meticulously organized into four vertical columns by bold red divider lines. In total, twelve figures—representing <term>priests</term> <note>often called *yaha yahui* or 'fire-serpent' priests in Mixtec culture</note> or perhaps deified ancestors—are depicted in profile. Each figure is heavily adorned in ritual regalia, signaling their specific roles and divine associations.

#### The First Column (Left)
The three figures in this column wear elaborate animal masks, including a **coyote** and a **jaguar**. They carry <term>ceremonial staffs</term> <note>sacred wands used to mark status and authority during religious rites</note>. Their bodies are painted in ochre and red, and they wear sandals signifying their high rank.

#### The Second Column
These three figures are distinguished by their massive **feathered headdresses**, likely fashioned from the iridescent green plumage of the quetzal bird. They wear circular jade earspools and necklaces, symbolizing "preciousness" and the life-giving properties of water and vegetation.

#### The Third Column
The figures in the third column continue the procession. One wears a bird-like mask with a curved beak, possibly representing a wind or solar deity. The use of **turquoise** and **terracotta** pigments here creates a rhythmic visual flow, common in the <term>Mixtec-Puebla style</term> <note>a sophisticated artistic tradition characterized by bold outlines and standardized symbolic imagery</note>.

#### The Fourth Column (Right)
The final three figures complete the sequence. They hold their ceremonial wands forward, moving in unison toward a destination or event described on the following pages. Their attire includes intricate capes and loincloths, each decorated with geometric patterns that may represent specific lineages or towns.

# Page 72

[This page consists entirely of Mixtec pictographic glyphs and contains no alphabetical text. The imagery depicts a series of place signs <term>toponyms: symbols used to represent the names of specific geographic locations</term> represented by "hill" or "mountain" glyphs. Each is associated with specific iconographic elements—such as animals, deities, sacrificial tools, and architectural structures—likely recording historical lineages or the extent of geographical territories.]

<note>In Mixtec codices, history was "read" through these complex images. A hill symbol modified by another icon (like a bird or a spear) tells the reader exactly which town or mountain is being discussed.</note>

The page is organized into distinct panels divided by **vertical red lines**, which guide the reader through the narrative. The following elements are featured prominently:

*   A **jaguar-patterned hill** <note>The jaguar was a symbol of supreme power, the night sun, and the elite warrior classes</note>.
*   Figures standing upon **stepped platforms**, representing temples or administrative seats.
*   **Mountain signs** paired with sacrificial spears, possibly indicating conquered territories.
*   Various animal motifs, including **eagles** and **dogs**, which may represent the names of specific lords or ethnic groups.

<margin>These sequences likely document the political foundations of a lineage or the specific boundaries of a kingdom's influence.</margin>

<summary>This page presents a complex series of Mixtec pictograms representing "place signs" (toponyms) and historical events, using symbols like jaguar-patterned hills and sacrificial spears to map out territorial or genealogical history. It serves as a visual record of political and religious geography in pre-Hispanic Mexico.</summary>

# Page 71

[This page is a purely pictorial manuscript from a Mixtec codex. It contains no alphabetical text. The following is a descriptive translation of the visual narrative.]

The page is organized into twelve distinct scenes, primarily featuring <term>hill signs (original: *tepetl* or *yucu*)</term> <note>These bell-shaped icons are the standard Mesoamerican way of representing a town, kingdom, or specific geographic location.</note> used to denote place names. These are accompanied by various ritual and calendaric symbols.

### The Top Row

*   A striped feline, likely a **jaguar**, is shown seated upon a stepped, patterned platform. <note>The jaguar often symbolized royalty or martial prowess in Mixtec culture.</note>
*   A hill sign featuring a geometric **fret pattern** <note>Also known as *xicalcoliuhqui*, representing waves or clouds.</note> and a spear or <term>atlatl (spear-thrower)</term> dart.
*   A hill sign containing a seated figure performing a **ritual**, with a serpent head symbol to the right.
*   A hill sign topped with a bundle of feathers or sacred grass, accompanied by **five circular markers**. <note>These circles typically represent numbers in the calendar or a count of years.</note>

### The Middle Row

*   A scene depicting a figure interacting with a complex **deity or ritual bundle** atop a hill sign.
*   A hill sign with a **bird** (possibly a parrot) perched on top and another bird below, accompanied by **seven circular markers**.
*   A hill sign featuring a **flowering or fruiting tree**. <note>Trees often represent the lineage or the "Tree of Life" from which the first Mixtec ancestors emerged.</note>
*   A white hill sign adorned with a **sun-like or fan-like ornament**.

### The Bottom Row

*   A hill sign with a **serpent head** emerging from it and a ritual staff placed to the side.
*   A rectangular platform containing four pointed elements, which may represent **spikes or cacti**.
*   A hill sign containing a small symbol or **glyph** inside, accompanied by a feathered staff.
*   A hill sign adorned with multiple **feather bundles**, accompanied by a serpent head and **five circular markers**.

<summary>This page serves as a "map" of twelve specific locations or "Place Signs," likely recording the territories or conquests of a Mixtec ruling lineage. The combination of geographic markers with ritual objects and numbers suggests a record of both space and sacred time.</summary>

<keywords>Mixtec codex, hill signs, place names, jaguar, ritual bundles, Mesoamerican iconography, calendar markers, genealogy</keywords>

### Register 1 (Upper Tier)

**Scene 1: Ritual Priest**
A figure painted entirely in black, a color often signifying a priest or a specific deity in Mixtec culture, moves toward the right. He wears a white loincloth and carries two ornate ritual vessels, likely incense burners or drinking cups, in his hands. He is adorned with an elaborate feathered headdress and a decorative back-piece. <note>The black paint suggests the priest is performing a high-status or underworld-related ritual.</note>

---

**Scene 2: Encounter of Two Warriors**
Two figures in highly decorative warrior regalia face each other across a central space.
- The figure on the left holds a <term>spear-thrower <note>original Nahuatl: atlatl</note></term> and a shield. He wears a bird-themed headdress and a decorated tunic. Above him is a name or date glyph consisting of a human head and numerical markers.
- The figure on the right holds a shield and a spear. He wears a coyote or wolf-head headdress. Above him is a date glyph featuring a serpent head and seven dots, representing the date **7 Serpent**.
They stand upon a platform decorated with a traditional <term>stepped-fret design <note>original Nahuatl: xicalcoliuhqui</note></term>.

---

**Scene 3: Lord 8 Deer**
On the far right, a warrior stands holding a long red spear and a circular shield with feather fringe. He wears an elaborate feathered back-piece and headdress. Beside him is the date glyph **8 Deer** (a deer's head accompanied by eight dots). <note>This is almost certainly "8 Deer Jaguar Claw," the most famous Mixtec ruler and conqueror.</note>

---

### Register 2 (Lower Tier)

**Scene 4: Ritual at the Altar**
Two figures perform a ritual over a stepped altar or hearth where wood is burning.
- The figure on the left is associated with the glyph **4 Flower**.
- The figure on the right is associated with a name glyph.
They appear to be making offerings; a bundle of ritual items sits between them on the ground.

---

**Scene 5: <term>Place Signs <note>original Greek-derived: toponyms</note></term> and Attendants**
This section features two large "hill" glyphs representing specific geographical locations. <note>In Mixtec codices, a hill symbol combined with another image identifies a specific town or kingdom.</note>
- The upper hill glyph is topped by a predatory bird, possibly an eagle. 
- The lower hill glyph contains a colorful parrot within a rectangular frame.
To the right of these signs are two human figures:
- An upper figure stands holding a long, flowered staff.
- A smaller figure below, painted black, holds a small ritual effigy or animal.

# Mixtec Pictographic Narrative

## Column 1
* **Top:** Two temple-like platforms, each topped with a stylized tree or cactus motif. <note>In Mixtec codices, trees often function as "place signs," identifying specific towns or sacred locations by the type of vegetation shown.</note>
* **Middle:** A platform with horizontal red and white stripes, upon which rests a decapitated human head. <note>The red and white "candy-stripe" pattern is frequently associated with sacrificial sites or the "Altar of Stones."</note>
* **Bottom:** A large brown platform with a central black vertical column or <term>monolith</term> <note>a large, single upright block of stone often used as a monument or ritual marker</note>.

## Column 2
* **Top:** A noble figure in profile walking across a brown platform; black footprints indicate the path of travel. <note>The use of footprints is a common artistic convention in Mesoamerica to denote a journey, the passage of time, or a specific historical path taken by an individual.</note>
* **Middle:** A brown platform with three <term>flint knives</term> <note>original: *tecpatl*</note> embedded in the top surface. <note>Flint knives were essential ritual tools and often symbolized war or sacrifice.</note>
* **Bottom:** A circular <term>ceremonial shield</term> <note>original: *chimalli*</note> featuring a central bird—possibly an eagle or vulture—against a background divided into red, blue, and yellow quadrants.

## Column 3
* **Top:** A small enclosure containing a bird and a string of beads.
* **Middle:** A conical thatched structure, possibly a ritual hut or <term>sweatbath</term> <note>original: *temazcal*; these structures were used for physical and spiritual purification through steam</note>.
* **Bottom:** A ritual human sacrifice scene. A dark-skinned priest performs an extraction on a victim laid across a stone. Below the ritual, a jaguar and a <term>feathered serpent</term> <note>a primary deity representing the sky and earth, known as 9 Wind in Mixtec culture</note> appear on a long platform decorated with circular motifs.

## Column 4
* **Top:** A large seated figure, possibly the famous ruler <term>Lord 8 Deer "Jaguar Claw"</term> <note>a powerful 11th-century Mixtec expansionist king who united the three Mixtec regions</note>, wearing an elaborate headdress and holding a ritual vessel and staff.
* **Bottom:** Decorative <term>glyphs</term> <note>pictorial symbols representing words or ideas</note> including a serpent head and a string of jade beads. <note>Jade beads typically symbolize "preciousness" or "life-force" in this artistic tradition.</note>

<summary>This page depicts a series of ritual and political events, including human sacrifice and purification rites, centered around a prominent noble figure, likely Lord 8 Deer "Jaguar Claw." It utilizes standard Mixtec iconographic symbols such as footprints for travel and striped platforms for sacrificial locations.</summary>
<keywords>Mixtec, Codex, Lord 8 Deer, ceremonial shield, flint knives, human sacrifice, sweatbath, Mesoamerican iconography, feathered serpent, Jaguar Claw</keywords>

# Page 68

->**The Ritual Foundation of Sacred Sites**<-

This page presents a series of pictographic narratives detailing the ceremonial activities of several divine or high-status figures. As is characteristic of Mixtec codices, the "text" is read through the positions, attire, and accompanying symbols of the figures depicted.

### The Upper Register

On the upper left, a **sacred figure or priest** is shown in elaborate ritual attire. He holds a staff of authority and stands atop a green platform. Beneath him is a <term>glyph<note>A symbolic character or figure used in Mesoamerican writing systems to represent names, dates, or places.</note></term> depicting a fish head, which likely serves as a <term>toponym<note>A place-name sign.</note></term> identifying this specific location.

To the right, a **warrior or deity** appears in the guise of a jaguar, wearing the animal's pelt. He brandishes a <term>macuahuitl<note>A wooden sword-club embedded with razor-sharp obsidian blades; the primary shock weapon of Mesoamerican warriors.</note></term> and carries a shield, standing on a platform decorated with a woven mat pattern—a symbol of authority and "the seat of power." A serpent's head glyph accompanies this scene.

Dominating the center-right is a **tiered temple or shrine**. It features a traditional thatched roof and a red lintel over the doorway. The structure rests on a wide base decorated with the <term>xicalcoliuhqui<note>original: "xicalcoliuhqui" — A ubiquitous Mesoamerican decorative motif also known as the "stepped fret" or "greca," often symbolizing clouds, waves, or the serpent.</note></term> pattern. This architectural drawing signifies a specific holy site where the following rituals take place.

In the upper far right, a **ritual bundle** <note>These "mummy bundles" often contained the remains of ancestors or sacred objects and were treated as living oracles.</note> wrapped in diagonally striped, multi-colored cloth is placed upon a tiered altar.

### The Lower Register

In the lower-left section, a **dark-skinned figure** is depicted. This coloration likely represents black ritual body paint, identifying the figure as a priest or a deity associated with the night or the underworld. He holds a ritual staff and stands upon an ornate red and brown altar.

Beside him, another **warrior or deity** dressed in a vibrant yellow and blue costume holds a spear and shield. This figure is accompanied by a lizard glyph and a small sacrificial bundle, possibly indicating a specific date in the 260-day ritual calendar.

Finally, on the lower right, a **lord or high-status individual** wearing a magnificent feather headdress and carrying a large shield stands before a ceremonial platform. The intricate geometric patterns on the platform suggest a specific courtyard or plaza of importance.

<summary>This page depicts a series of ritual foundations and warrior-deity manifestations centered around a thatched temple and various ceremonial platforms. It records the sacred history of Mixtec lords or gods through complex iconography rather than alphabetical text.</summary>

<keywords>Mixtec codex, macuahuitl, xicalcoliuhqui, jaguar warrior, ritual bundle, Mesoamerican temple, toponym, obsidian sword</keywords>

# Page 65

This page presents a complex ritual narrative arranged in vertical columns, separated by traditional red lines. Here we see eleven figures, likely <term>Deities</term> <note>original Mixtec: *Dzahui* or *Ñuhu*—beings associated with rain, earth, and the sacred landscape</note> or venerated <term>Ancestors</term>.

### The Ritual Assembly

The figures are depicted in vibrant colors, wearing the elaborate regalia characteristic of the <term>Postclassic</term> <note>The final period of Mesoamerican history before Spanish contact, roughly 900–1521 CE</note> era in <term>Mesoamerica</term> <note>The cultural region encompassing central/southern Mexico and parts of Central America</note>.

*   **Headdresses:** Each figure wears a magnificent crown of feathers, signifying their high status or divine nature.
*   **Facial Paint:** The intricate patterns on their faces identify their specific roles, names, or divine lineages.
*   **Regalia:** Many figures carry ceremonial shields and staffs, symbols of both political power and sacred office.

-> **The Sacred Columns** <-

The scene is organized into columns to guide the reader through the ceremony, a common technique in <term>Pictography</term> <note>A system of writing using pictures to represent words or ideas</note>:

| Column Position | Content | Significance |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Left | Sacred Ancestors | Figures representing the foundational lineage of the community. |
| Middle | Ritual Altars | Structures used for making offerings and connecting with the divine. |
| Right | Divine Deities | The gods who oversee the ceremony or receive the offerings. |

<margin>The red lines serve as "reading paths," indicating the order in which the narrative should be viewed.</margin>

The ritual structures, or altars, are shown as platforms where the figures interact with the sacred realm. These <term>Iconographic</term> <note>The visual language of symbols and images</note> elements allow the reader to identify the specific type of ceremony being performed, such as a marriage, a dedication, or a prayer for rain.

<summary>This page depicts a ritual gathering of eleven divine or ancestral figures organized into structured columns, highlighting the importance of ceremonial regalia and sacred offerings in Mixtec culture.</summary>
<keywords>Mixtec, codex, ritual, deities, ancestors, Postclassic, iconography, Mesoamerica, ceremony</keywords>

# Codex Page 64

---

This page consists of twelve distinct iconographic vignettes <note>vignette: a small, self-contained illustration that conveys a specific scene or idea</note> arranged in four vertical columns, separated by prominent red vertical bars. The scenes depict various deities or lords in ritual poses, accompanied by specific symbolic glyphs <note>glyph: a symbolic character used in Mesoamerican writing to represent names, dates, or objects</note>.

**Column 1**

Beneath him, two further nobles are depicted in a kneeling posture, indicating a gesture of respect or subservience. Their garments are intricately patterned with geometric designs that denote their specific city-state or "Altépetl."

This section depicts a series of seated figures facing one another, suggesting a formal council or the negotiation of a marriage alliance. The central figure, **Lord 5 Movement**, holds a flowered staff, a symbol of peaceful oratory and diplomatic skill.

The final column anchors the genealogical list to specific locations. A series of glyphs represent "Place Signs":

# Page 58: The Assembly of the Divine

->**The Sacred Order of the Pantheon**<-

This page is meticulously divided into eight distinct scenes, or vignettes, by vibrant red framing lines. Each compartment captures a moment of divine significance, featuring mythological figures and deities seated in profile. These beings are not merely portraits; they are complex symbols of the Mixtec universe, dressed in elaborate ritual costumes that signify their status and power.

### The Divine Figures and their Attributes

Each figure wears a **zoomorphic** <term>Zoomorphic: Characterized by animal forms; in this context, it refers to headdresses that grant the wearer the attributes of the animal represented.</term> headdress, predominantly featuring the forms of birds and serpents. These animals connect the earthly realm with the celestial and subterranean worlds.

The figures are distinguished by their striking skin colors:
*   **Deep Purple and Red:** Often associated with the rising sun and the direction of the East.
*   **Yellow:** Frequently linked to the West or the feminine aspects of the earth.
*   **White and Black:** Often representing the North, the cold, or the realm of the ancestors.

<note>Color in Mixtec codices is never purely decorative; it serves as a directional and theological code, identifying the deity's origin and sphere of influence.</note>

### Calendrical Notations and Ritual Objects

Accompanying each divine figure are specific calendrical symbols. These consist of stylized animal heads paired with a series of colored dots.

| Vignette Position | Primary Iconography | Significance |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Upper Row | Figures on stepped thrones | These represent established authority or deities in their celestial palaces. |
| Lower Row | Ritual vessels with faces | A <term>Conical Vessel: A ritual container, likely used for offerings such as incense or fermented pulque.</term> |

The presence of the **stepped base** or throne indicates that these figures are being honored or are presiding over a specific ritual cycle. The <insert>dots and animal heads</insert> provide the specific ritual dates, likely part of the 260-day <term>Tonalpohualli: The sacred calendar cycle used to determine auspicious days for rituals and governance.</term>

<margin>The artist used a thick red pigment to define the boundaries of each scene, ensuring the sacred energies of one deity do not bleed into the next.</margin>

->*The interplay of color, costume, and calendar on this page suggests a manual for ritual practitioners to ensure that the correct offerings were made to the correct deity at the precise time dictated by the stars.*<-

<note>The "stepped base" seen here is a common architectural motif in Mesoamerican art, mirroring the shape of the great temple-pyramids where these rituals were enacted in the physical world.</note>

### The Sacred Figures and Their Names
The panels depict six figures, either seated or kneeling, dressed in elaborate **ritual regalia**. Each individual is identified by a unique **calendar name** <note>In the Mixtec tradition, a person's name was determined by their birth date in the 260-day sacred calendar.</note> consisting of a day-sign and a specific number of dots.

The figures wear intricate headdresses and feathered garments. <margin>The detail in the headdresses denotes the specific rank and ethnic identity of the individuals.</margin> They are shown seated upon woven reed mats (*petates*), which were the traditional "thrones" of Mesoamerican rulers, symbolizing their right to govern.

In the lower-left corner, a **temple or palace structure** is illustrated. <term>Place Sign</term>: In Mixtec codices, buildings are not just architecture; they are "place signs" that identify specific towns, mountains, or valleys through the symbols attached to them. This structure likely represents the location where the depicted ritual or meeting took place.

-> **The Assembly of the Noble Ancestors** <-

In the upper register of the manuscript, various divine and human figures are depicted seated upon ornate platforms. Each figure is identified by their **calendrical name**, represented by a series of circular dots indicating a number and a specific animal or object head representing the day.

In the middle section, separated by distinct red lines, two figures are engaged in a formal exchange across a central structure. This structure represents a **temple or palace**, decorated with intricate geometric patterns.

*   **The Hill of the Twisted Stone** <margin>Located at the eastern boundary of the territory</margin>
*   **The Mountain of the Jewel** <note>This likely refers to a site of economic or mineral importance to the dynasty</note>
*   **The Temple of the Sky** <insert>added to denote a place of recent conquest</insert>

The page is filled with **calendrical signs**—dots and icons—that anchor these meetings and places within the 260-day ritual cycle, ensuring that the history of the lineage is forever tied to the sacred passage of time.

### Folio 48

This page serves as a historical ledger of territorial expansion, recorded through the sophisticated pictographic system of the Mixtec people. The narrative is read through a series of <term>toponyms</term> <note>from the Greek 'topos' for place and 'onoma' for name; these are symbols representing specific geographic locations</note>.

The primary symbols on this page are **hill glyphs** <note>original Mixtec: "yucu"</note>. In Mixtec iconography, a bell-shaped hill represents a town, city, or kingdom. On this page, nearly every hill is shown **pierced by a spear or arrow**, which is the standard artistic shorthand indicating that the location has been conquered in battle or brought under political submission.

The specific towns or regions identified by the icons within the hills include:
*   **The Place of the Hand**
*   **The Place of the Blue Mask**
*   **The Place of the Ceramic Vessel**
*   **The Place of the Animal Skins and Spindles** <note>Spindles often denote centers of weaving or textile production.</note>
*   **The Place of the Deer Antlers**
*   **The Place of the Vulture**

Interspersed among these conquered territories are individual glyphs that likely represent specific dates or the names of the presiding lords:
*   A **Rabbit head** and a **Coyote head**, both accompanied by strings of beads <note>The beads represent numerical values, identifying these as specific days in the 260-day ritual calendar, such as "8 Rabbit" or "10 Coyote."</note>.
*   A **human skull**, possibly indicating a site of ancestral remains or a place of sacrifice.
*   An **ornate feathered structure**, representing a temple or a high-ranking ceremonial office.

The vibrant colors and precise placement of these icons functioned as a public record of the lineage's power and the extent of their domain.

# Codex Manuscript Page 47

This page from a Mixtec <term>codex</term> <note>A traditional Mesoamerican folding book, typically made of deer hide or bark paper coated with white lime.</note> features eight distinct pictorial scenes arranged in a grid-like narrative, separated by thick red guidelines. The illustrations utilize a characteristic palette of red, turquoise, ochre, and brown to depict various ritual activities, offerings, and mythological figures.

### Upper Register

*   **Top Left**: A large, stylized <term>place-sign</term> <note>In Mixtec writing, locations are represented by combining a base 'hill' or 'platform' glyph with specific qualifiers.</note> or entity glyph shaped like a blue and brown reptilian creature with multiple eyes along its back. *   **Upper Far Right**: A figure in a purple garment holds a small jaguar. Below them is a severed human head resting on a stepped platform, suggesting a scene of sacrifice or conquest.

*   **Lower Middle**: A yellow-skinned figure walking and holding a small white bundle, possibly a deity image or a newborn. Above them is a serpent head calendar glyph with four brown circles (4-Serpent).
*   **Lower Right-Center**: A red-skinned figure gestures toward a stylized cross-shaped floral or butterfly-like symbol on the ground.
*   **Lower Far Right**: A figure wearing an ornate avian mask stands near a feathered serpent head. At the bottom is a stylized flowering tree or plant emerging from a decorative stepped platform.

To the right, a small green **lizard** descends a vertical row of seven yellow dots. A cross-shaped glyph in red and blue, likely representing the four cardinal directions or a specific star, is situated in the upper right corner.

# Sacred Emblems and Place-Names

->**The Ritual Perforator**<-

The upper portion of this page features a prominent <term>bone-awl</term> <note>original: "nduvua" — a sharp instrument typically fashioned from deer bone</note>. This tool is a primary symbol of ritual bloodletting and penitence among the nobility. The glyph is rendered in deep red and green pigments, entwined with a decorative gold band and a circular bead ornament. This ornamentation indicates that the tool is not merely a functional object, but a sacred heirloom or a marker of high status within a priestly lineage.

->**The Glyph of the Jaw**<-

Below the ritual instrument is a <term>toponym</term> <note>a graphic symbol representing the name of a town or geographic feature</note>. This specific sign depicts a stylized jaw or mountain-like structure.

*   **Teeth:** The structure contains sharp, red-tipped teeth, which may serve as a phonetic indicator for "stone" or "hard place."
*   **The Scroll:** A decorative scroll emerges from the side, a common convention in Mixtec art used to denote speech, breath, or the "name" of a place.
*   **Significance:** This combination likely names a specific location such as "Mountain of the Jaw" or "Place of the Teeth," acting as a landmark for the historical events recorded in the surrounding manuscript <note>In Mixtec iconography, the "open mouth" of the earth often represents a cave or a sacred entrance to the underworld.</note>.

<summary>This page presents two foundational Mixtec glyphs: a ritual bone-awl symbolizing sacrifice and a toponymic jaw-mountain representing a specific geographic location. These images illustrate the intersection of religious duty and territorial identity in indigenous record-keeping.</summary>
